Global and local CLTs for linear spectral statistics of general sample covariance matrices when the dimension is much larger than the sample size with applications
Xiucai Ding, Zhenggang Wang

Abstract
In this paper, under the assumption that the dimension is much larger than the sample size, i.e., p ≍ n α , α > 1 , p\asymp n^{\alpha},\alpha>1, we consider the (unnormalized) sample covariance matrices Q = Σ 1 / 2 ​ X ​ X ∗ ​ Σ 1 / 2 Q=\Sigma^{1/2}XX^{*}\Sigma^{1/2} , where X = ( x i ​ j ) X=(x_{ij}) is a p × n p\times n random matrix with centered i.i.d entries whose variances are ( p n ) − 1 / 2 (pn)^{-1/2} , and Σ \Sigma is the deterministic population covariance matrix. We establish two classes of central limit theorems (CLTs) for the linear spectral statistics (LSS) for Q , Q, the global CLTs on the macroscopic scales and the local CLTs on the mesoscopic scales. We prove that the LSS converge to some Gaussian processes whose mean and covariance functions depending on Σ \Sigma , the ratio p / n p/n and the test functions, can be identified explicitly on both macroscopic and mesoscopic scales. We also show that even though the global CLTs depend on the fourth cumulant of x i ​ j , x_{ij}, the local CLTs do not. Based on these results, we propose two classes of statistics for testing the structures of Σ , \Sigma, the global statistics and the local statistics, and analyze their superior power under general local alternatives. To our best knowledge, the local LSS testing statistics which do not rely on the fourth moment of x i ​ j , x_{ij}, is used for the first time in hypothesis testing while the literature mostly uses the global statistics and requires the prior knowledge of the fourth cumulant. Numerical simulations also confirm the accuracy and powerfulness of our proposed statistics and illustrate better performance compared to the existing methods in the literature.



1
Introduction

Covariance matrices are fundamental objects in multivariate analysis
[
2
]
and high dimensional statistics
[
62
]
. Many statistical methodologies and techniques rely on the knowledge of the structure of the covariance matrix, to
name but a few, Principal Component Analysis, Discriminant Analysis and
Cluster Analysis. In order to correctly apply these tools, people usually need to conduct hypothesis testings to understand the population covariance structures. An important problem of significant interest is to test

\mathbf{H}_{0}:\Sigma=\Sigma_{0},

(1.1)

where
\Sigma
is the population covariance matrix and
\Sigma_{0}
is some pre-given positive definite matrix. Even though many classic statistics including the likelihood ratio statistic and Nagao statistic
[
2
,
51
]
are useful in the setting when the dimension
p
is fixed and the sample size goes to infinity, they lose their validity when
p
diverges with
n.
To overcome the curse of high dimensionality, many modified or novel statistics have been proposed in the last decades, to name but a few
[
19
,
64
,
21
,
67
,
49
,
17
,
3
,
57
,
33
,
37
,
53
,
1
,
28
,
58
,
22
]
. For a comprehensive review, we refer the readers to
[
18
]
.

We point out that the aforementioned statistics can be roughly divided into two classes. The first classes of the statistics rely on some
U
-statistics like
[
19
,
1
,
22
,
17
,
49
]
. Even though
U
-statistics can be made dimension-free, there exist several disadvantages. First, it is computationally expensive. Second, it usually requires the prior knowledge of the fourth cumulant of the random variables. Third, it usually needs a relatively strong alternative to make the statistics powerful. The second classes of the statistics utilize the linear spectral statistics (LSS) of some sample covariance matrices like
[
28
,
21
,
53
,
64
,
58
,
33
,
67
,
3
]
. The statistics’ straightforward constructions make it highly computationally efficient. However, all the existing literature requires the prior knowledge of the fourth cumulant of the random vectors and most of the existing literature focuses on a specific regime that
p
and
n
are comparably large and do not apply to the setting when
p
is much larger than
n
.

In this paper, we aim to propose some LSS based statistics to simultaneously address several aforementioned challenges. First, the proposed statistics are accurate in the regime that
p
is much large than
n
in the sense that

p\asymp n^{\alpha},\ \text{for some constant}\ 1<\alpha<\infty.

(1.2)

Second, the proposed statistics do not necessarily rely on the cumulants of the random vectors. Finally, the proposed statistics should be powerful under weak local alternatives.

1.1
Some related results on LSS of sample covariance matrices

In this section, we first pause to give a brief review of the literature on results related to linear spectral statistics especially in the context of sample covariance matrices. In the literature of random matrix theory, two types of CLTs for LSS have been established: the global (a.k.a. macroscopic) CLTs and the local (a.k.a mesoscopic) CLTs. The global CLTs are computed on the global scales so that all the eigenvalues will be utilized, while the local CLTs are calculated on the local scales that only a relatively small part of the eigenvalues will be counted.

Most of the results are established assuming that
p
and
n
are comparably large. On the global scales, the CLTs for linear eigenvalue statistics were first studied in
[
34
]
for Wishart matrices. Later on, the CLTs for linear eigenvalues statistics with analytic test functions for general sample covariance matrices were studied in
[
8
]
. Then the regularity conditions on the test functions were weakened under various settings in
[
6
,
48
,
54
,
52
]
. The ideas and results were also applied to study various hypothesis testing problems in
[
46
,
65
,
64
,
21
,
41
,
16
,
32
,
3
,
33
,
9
,
40
,
68
,
66
,
67
,
31
,
59
,
39
,
71
]
. We point out that the ideas have also been borrowed to study other types of random matrices related to sample covariance matrices, for example, separable sample covariance matrices
[
4
,
42
]
, sample correlation matrices
[
63
,
69
,
29
]
and Kendall’s tau correlation matrices
[
45
]
. Furthermore, in addition to the eigenvalue LSS, the eigenvector LSS of the sample covariance matrices have also been studied in
[
7
,
24
,
60
,
61
]
. Much less work has been done on the local scales. To our best knowledge, even though lots of works have been done related Wigner matrices
[
30
,
36
,
43
,
44
]
, fewer concern the sample covariance matrices except for
[
43
,
61
]
.

Under our concerned regime (
1.2
), to our best awareness, the very limited existing works are all global CLTs
[
10
,
20
,
53
]
. Moreover, instead of analyzing the sample covariance matrices, they work with a normalized sample covariance matrices

A=\mathsf{c}_{1}X^{*}\Sigma X-\mathsf{c}_{2}\sqrt{p/n}I,

(1.3)

for some constants
\mathsf{c}_{1}
and
\mathsf{c}_{2}.
On the local scales, there does not exist any work. Motivated by these, our current work aims to fill these gaps by establishing both global and local CLTs under the setup (
1.2
) considering the following more standard (unnormalized) sample covariance matrix

Q=\Sigma^{1/2}XX^{*}\Sigma^{1/2},

(1.4)

where
X=(x_{ij})
is a
p\times n
random matrix with centered i.i.d entries whose variances are
(pn)^{-1/2}
, and
\Sigma
is the deterministic population covariance matrix.

1.2
An overview of our results and contributions

In this section, we provide a rough overview of our results and contributions. One purpose of this paper is to generalize both the global and local CLTs for
p\asymp n
to the ultra high dimensional setting (
1.1
). More concretely, we will study the LSS for the sample covariance matrix
Q
in (
1.4
) on both the global scales and local scales. We first show that the limiting ESD (LSD) of
Q
still follows a deformed Marchenco-Pastur (MP) law as in the spirit of
[
14
]
which only handles
\Sigma=I.
Even though the edges of the LSD diverge, it is supported on a single interval and the length is bounded once
\Sigma
is a bounded positive definite matrix; see Lemma
2.5
. This is quite different from the comparable setting
p\asymp n
that people usually need additional regularity conditions on
\Sigma
and the LSD may support on several disjoint intervals
[
35
]
.

For CLTs on the global scales, we prove that the joint distribution of LSS indexed by different test functions converge to some Gaussian processes whose mean and covariance functions can be identified explicitly and depend on the fourth cumulant of
x_{ij}
; see Theorem
3.2
. The literature mostly focuses on studying CLTs under the regime
p\asymp n
with stronger assumptions on the test functions, for example see
[
62
]
for a review. The only exceptions are
[
10
,
20
,
53
]
where the authors focus on a normalized sample covariance matrix in (
1.3
). First, the LSD of
A
follows Wigner’s semicircle law instead of MP law. Second, to facilitate statistical applications, they usually require stronger assumptions on
\alpha
or
\Sigma
. For example,
[
10
,
20
]
assumes
\Sigma=I
and
[
53
]
needs
\alpha\geq 2.
For CLTs on the local scales, we prove that the joint distribution of the LSS is also Gaussian. However, the mean and covariance functions do not depend on the fourth cumulant of
x_{ij};
see Theorem
3.7
. On the local scales, the most relevant work in the literature is
[
43
]
which only handles a single test function and requires
p\asymp n.
To our best knowledge, it is the first time that the local CLTs are established under the regime (
1.2
) and for multiple functions.

To ease applications and numerical calculations, we further simplify the formulas of the mean and covariance functions for some commonly used test functions in Corollaries
3.5
and
3.9
.
The theoretical results are not only interesting and natural on their own, they are also highly motivated by the statistical inference problem (
1.1
). In the related literature, people usually use global CLTs to construct statistics to test (
1.1
), see
[
62
]
for the
p\asymp n
setting and
[
53
]
for (
1.2
) with
\alpha\geq 2.
A disadvantage is that people need to know the fourth cumulant of
x_{ij}
in order to apply these tests. Motivated by this challenge, we propose two classes of testing statistics, global statistics and local statistics, in Section
4.1
where the local testing statistics are moment free in the sense that it does not depend on fourth cumulant of
x_{ij}
. These statistics are not only accurate as in Corollary
4.2
but also powerful as in Corollary
4.3
under weak local alternatives.

Finally, our proof strategies rely on the devices of cumulant expansions, Helffer-Sjöstrand formula and Wick’s probability theorem which follow and generalize those of
[
43
,
61
]
. We refer the readers to Section
3.3
for more details. We point out that the key ingredients are the local laws (c.f. Theorem
5.5
) for
Q
under (
1.2
) which is of self-interest and can be used to study other problems like spiked sample covariance matrices in the regime (
1.2
).

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the model and the asymptotic global laws. In Section
3
, we present our main results and outline the strategies and for the proofs. Section
4
discusses the statistical applications. The remainder of the paper is devoted to the details of the proof. In Section
5
, we summarize and provide some of the basic tools which will be used in the proofs. In Section
6
, we prove the CLTs for the resolvents which is an intermediate result for proving our final CLTs. In Section
7
, we prove the main theorems and in Section
8
, we prove the corollaries. Finally, Appendix
A
is devoted to the proof of the local laws and Appendix
B
proves other auxiliary lemmas.

Conventions and notations.
Throughout the paper, we always use
n
as the fundamental large parameter, and write
p\equiv p(n)
. For an
n\times n
symmetric matrix
H
, its empirical spectral distribution (ESD) is defined as
\mu_{H}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(H)},
where
\delta
is the Dirac’s delta function and
\{\lambda_{i}(H)\}
are the eigenvalues of
H.
For any probability measure
\nu
defined on
\mathbb{R},
its Stieltjes transform is defined as

m_{\nu}(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\nu(x),

where
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:=\{E+\mathrm{i}\eta:E\in\mathbb{R},\eta>0\}.

Acknowledgments.
The authors are supported by NSF-DMS 2113489 and a grant from UC Davis COVID-19
Research Accelerator Funding Track.

2
The model and asymptotic laws

We consider the sample covariance matrix
Q
in (
1.4
) and its eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}>0=\lambda_{n+1}=\cdots=\lambda_{p}.
Throughout the paper, to avoid repetition, we impose the following assumptions on
\Sigma
and
X.

Assumption 2.1
.

We assume that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1). For the
p\times n
matrix
X=(x_{ij}),1\leq i\leq p,1\leq j\leq n,
we assume that they are i.i.d. real random variables satisfying

\mathbb{E}x_{ij}=0,\quad\mathbb{E}x_{ij}^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{pn}}.

(2.1)

Moreover, for all
k\in\mathbb{N},
we assume that there exists some constant
C_{k}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left|(np)^{1/4}x_{ij}\right|^{k}\leqslant C_{k}.

(2.2)

(2). For the
p\times p
deterministic population covariance matrix
\Sigma,
we assume it is diagonal such that
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}\left(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{p}\right).
Moreover, for some small constant
0<\tau<1,

\tau\leq\sigma_{p}\leq\sigma_{p-1}\leq\cdots\leq\sigma_{1}\leq\tau^{-1}.

(3). For the dimensionality, we assume that (
1.2
) holds.

Remark 2.2
.

We provide some remarks on Assumption
2.1
. First, in (
2.1
), inspired by
[
14
]
, we choose the scaling
(pn)^{-1/2}
to deal with the regime (
1.2
) that
p
is much larger than
n.
From the viewpoint of multivariate statistics, it is different from the
n^{-1}
scaling used in the low dimensional regime that
\alpha<1

[
2
]
and the mean field regime
\alpha=1

[
62
]
. Second, motivated by the statistical applications, we mainly focus on the regime (
1.2
) when
\alpha>1
. However, our techniques and arguments can also be applied to the setting
0<\alpha\leq 1.
We will make this clear in our discussions from time to time. Third, the moment assumption (
2.2
) holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
can be easily generalized using the comparison argument as in
[
27
]
. Since this is not the focus of the paper, we will not pursue these generalizations. Finally, the diagonal assumption of
\Sigma,
on the one hand, can improve the interpretability of our results in the context of statistical inference. On the other hand, it can simplify our technical arguments. Such an assumption can be removed with additional technical efforts; see Remark
3.4
for more details.

In what follows, we first summarize the results on the asymptotic law of the ESD of
Q
. Since its nonzero eigenvalues are identical to those of
\mathcal{Q}=X^{*}\Sigma X
, it is sufficient to focus on the asymptotic deterministic equivalent of ESD of
\mathcal{Q},
denoted as
\varrho,
which can be best formulated by its Stieltjes transform. Denote

\phi\equiv\phi_{n}:=\frac{p}{n},

(2.3)

and
\pi
be the ESD of
\Sigma,
i.e.,
\pi:=p^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\delta_{\sigma_{i}}.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\pi
be a compactly supported probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\phi>0
.
Then for each
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},
there exists a unique
m\equiv m(z)\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
satisfying

\frac{1}{m}=-z+\int\frac{\phi}{\phi^{1/2}x^{-1}+m}\pi(\mathrm{d}x).

(2.4)

Moreover,
m(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of a probability measure
\varrho
with support in
[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

The proof follows from arguments similar to the well-known results as in
[
35
, Lemma 2.2]
or
[
55
, Section 5]
. The only difference is that we use the new scaling (
2.1
). We omit further details.

∎

Remark 2.4
.

First, we point out that the deterministic quantities
m(z)
and its associated
\varrho
depend implicitly on
n.
Moreover, even though the current paper focuses on the regime (
1.2
),
the results of Lemma
2.3
hold for all
0<\alpha<\infty
in (
1.2
). Second, the support of
\varrho
generally depends on
n
and even grows with
n.
To see this, we consider the example when
\Sigma=I.
In this setting, we find that (
2.4
) can be rewritten as

m+\frac{1}{z+z\phi^{-1/2}m-\left(\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}\right)}=0.

Solving the above equation with the branch cut that
m(z)
is holomorphic in the upper half-plane and satisfies
m(z)\rightarrow 0
as
z\rightarrow\infty
, we find that the unique solution of the above equation satisfying
\operatorname{Im}m(z)>0
for
\operatorname{Im}z>0
is

m(z)=\frac{\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}-z+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\left(z-\gamma_{-}\right)\left(\gamma_{+}-z\right)}}{2\phi^{-1/2}z}.

Using the inversion formula
[
5
]
, we find that under (
1.2
)

\varrho(\mathrm{d}x):=\frac{\sqrt{\phi}}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{\left[\left(x-\gamma_{-}\right)\left(\gamma_{+}-x\right)\right]_{+}}}{x}\mathrm{~d}x,

(2.5)

where
\gamma_{\pm}:=\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}\pm 2.
These coincide with the results in equations (2.4)-(2.7) of
[
14
]
.

According to Lemma
2.3
, we find that
m\equiv m(z)
can also be characterized as the unique solution of the equation

z=f(m),\quad\operatorname{Im}m>0,

(2.6)

where we define

f(x):=-\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{x+s_{i}^{-1}}=-\frac{1}{x}+\frac{\phi^{1/2}}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{1+\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}x},

(2.7)

where
s_{i}=\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}
. In what follows, we will show that the properties of
\varrho
can be understood via the analysis of
f
. As in
[
35
]
, we see that it is convenient to extend the domain of
f
to the real projective line
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. Clearly,
f
is smooth on the
p+1
open intervals of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
defined through

I_{1}:=\left(-s_{1}^{-1},0\right),\quad I_{i}:=\left(-s_{i}^{-1},-s_{i-1}^{-1}\right)\quad(i=2,\ldots,p),\quad I_{0}:=\overline{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}\bar{I}_{i}.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds, when
n
is sufficiently large, for
f(x)
defined in (
2.7
), there will be two critical points, denoted as
x_{1}
and
x_{2},
where
x_{1}\in I_{1}
and
x_{2}\in I_{0}.
Moreover, define
\gamma_{+}:=f(x_{1})
and
\gamma_{-}:=f(x_{2}).
We have that
\gamma_{+}\geq\gamma_{-}>0
and

\operatorname{supp}\varrho\cap(0,\infty)=[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}].

Finally, we have that

\gamma_{+}-\gamma_{-}=\mathrm{O}(1),\ \operatorname{and}\ \gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}\asymp\phi^{1/2}.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.2
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

We provide two remarks regarding (
1.2
). First, analogous results of Lemma
2.5
for
\alpha=1
in (
1.2
) have been proved in Lemmas 2.4-2.6 of
[
35
]
. Our results in Lemma
2.5
demonstrate a structural difference on
\varrho
between the setting
\alpha=1
and
\alpha>1.
Especially, when
\alpha=1,
the function
f
may have an even number of more than two critical points so that the support of
f
may have several components as a union for several closed intervals; see Figure
1
for an illustration. Second, as discussed in Remark
2.4
, our proof of Lemma
2.5
can be easily generalized to study the setting
\alpha<1.
In this case, the results will be similar to those of
\alpha=1
in
[
35
]
. Especially, by introducing the multiset
\mathcal{C}\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}}
of critical points of
f
, using the conventions that a nondegenerate critical point is counted once and a degenerate critical point twice, we can show that
\left|\mathcal{C}\cap I_{0}\right|=\left|\mathcal{C}\cap I_{1}\right|=1
and
\left|\mathcal{C}\cap I_{i}\right|\in\{0,2\}
for
i=2,\ldots,q.
Consequently, we see that
|\mathcal{C}|=2q
is even. We denote by
x_{1}\geqslant x_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant x_{2q-1}
the
2q-1
critical points in
I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{p}
, and by
x_{2q}
the unique critical point in
I_{0}
. For
k=1,\ldots,2q
we define the critical values
a_{k}:=f\left(x_{k}\right)
. We can show that
a_{1}\geqslant a_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant a_{2q}\geq 0
and
a_{k}=\mathrm{O}(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}).
Consequently, this leads to
\operatorname{supp}\varrho\cap(0,\infty)=\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{q}\left[a_{2k},a_{2k-1}\right]\right)\cap(0,\infty).
For more details, we refer the readers to Appendix
B.2
.

(a)

\phi=0.6

(b)

\phi=100

Figure 1:
The densities and histograms for
\pi=0.5\delta_{1}+0.5\delta_{15}
for
\phi=0.6
and
\phi=100.
The densities are computed using the Stieltjes transform via (
2.4
) with the inversion formula of the Stieltjes transform that
\rho(x)=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\operatorname{Im}m(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)
and the histograms are obtained using (
1.4
) with
x_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(pn)^{-1/2}),n=800
via
500
Monte Carlo simulations. We can see that when
\phi=0.6
is smaller, we can have two separate bulk components in the support while
\phi=100
is larger we only have one bulk component.

Remark 2.7
.

Two remarks are in order. First, according to Lemma
2.5
, the edges of the support of
\varrho
can be determined if
\Sigma
is known. For example, when the ESD of
\Sigma
follows
\pi=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{b}
for some positive constants
a,b>0,
it is easy to see that

\gamma_{\pm}=\frac{(a+b)\phi^{1/2}}{2}\pm\sqrt{2(a^{2}+b^{2})}.

Second, as discussed in Section 3.2 of
[
15
]
, after properly being scaled and centered, the asymptotic law obtained from Lemma
2.3
has a natural connection with the Wigner’s semicircle law. In fact, using Lemma
5.1
below and a straightforward expansion, we see from (
2.4
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}=-z+\phi^{1/2}\int x\pi(\mathrm{d}x)-\int x^{2}\pi(\mathrm{d}x)m+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{-1/2}).

Especially, when
\pi(x)=\delta_{1},
we see that

\frac{1}{m}+m+z-(\phi^{1/2}+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{-1/2}))=0.

This is nothing but Wigner’s semicircle law centered at
\phi^{1/2}+\mathrm{O}(\phi^{-1/2})
which recovers the discussions of equations (3.16) and (3.17) of
[
15
]
at the cost of
\mathrm{O}(\phi^{-1/2}).

3
Main results

In this section, we prove the main results and sketch the proof strategies. We first prepare some notations. Fix some small constant
0<\tau<1,
we denote

\mathbf{R}\equiv\mathbf{R}(\tau):=\left\{E\in\mathbb{R}:\ |E-\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi)\phi^{1/2}|\leq\tau^{-1}\right\},\ \text{where}\ \mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi)=\int x\pi(\mathrm{d}x).

(3.1)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be the function space on some compact set
\mathbf{C}\subset\mathbb{R}
of continuous functions that have continuous first two derivatives. For some fixed integer
\mathsf{K}\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence of test functions
g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x),\cdots,g_{\mathsf{K}}(x)\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and some constants
E\in\mathbf{R},\eta_{0}>0
we define
f_{i}(x)\equiv f_{i}(x;E,\eta_{0}),1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K},

f_{i}(x)\equiv f_{i}(x;E,\eta_{0}):=g_{i}\left(\frac{x-E}{\eta_{0}}\right),\ 1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}.

(3.2)

Recall from Lemma
2.3
that
\varrho
is the asymptotic density of the limiting ESD. For
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K},
denote

Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{i}(\lambda_{j})-n\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)\mathrm{d}\varrho(x).

(3.3)

To ease our statements, following
[
11
, Definition 2.2]
, we use the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Two sequences of random vectors
\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{K}},\ n\geq 1,
are asymptotically equal in distribution, denoted by
\bm{x}_{n}\simeq\bm{y}_{n},
if they are tight (i.e., for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists a
D>0
such that
\sup_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|\bm{x}_{n}\|\geq D)\leq\epsilon
) and satisfy

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}h(\bm{x}_{n})-\mathbb{E}h(\bm{y}_{n})\right)=0,

for any bounded continuous function
h:\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{K}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

3.1
Global CLTs under (
1.2
)

Let
\kappa_{k}
be the
k
-th cumulant of
(pn)^{1/4}x_{ij}
in (
2.1
), given by
\kappa_{k}:=(-\mathrm{i})^{k}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log\mathbb{E}e^{\mathrm{i}t(pn)^{1/4}x_{ij}}|_{t=0}.
Moreover, for
m(z)
defined in (
2.4
), we denote

b(z):=b_{1}(z)+b_{2}(z),

(3.4)

where
b_{1}(z)
and
b_{2}(z)
are defined as

b_{1}(z):=\frac{{m}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{2{m}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{{m}^{\prime}(z)}{{m(z)}},\ b_{2}(z)=\kappa_{4}\left(\frac{m^{2}(z)m^{\prime\prime}(z)}{2{m^{\prime}(z)}^{2}}-m(z)\right).

(3.5)

Moreover, for any real number
x\in\mathbb{R},
we use the convention that

b_{1}^{\pm}(x)=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}b_{1}(x\pm\mathrm{i}\eta),\ b_{2}^{\pm}(x)=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}b_{2}(x\pm\mathrm{i}\eta),\ \ m^{\pm}(x)=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}m(x\pm\mathrm{i}\eta).

Armed with the above conventions, for
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\{+,-\},
we further define

\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right):=\kappa_{4}\phi\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{1}\partial x_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{\left(1+\phi^{-1/2}{m}^{\mathfrak{a}}(x_{1})\sigma_{i}\right)\left(1+\phi^{-1/2}{m}^{\mathfrak{b}}(x_{2})\sigma_{i}\right)}\right),

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right):=2\left(\frac{(m^{\mathfrak{a}})^{\prime}(x_{1})(m^{\mathfrak{b}})^{\prime}(x_{2})}{(m^{\mathfrak{a}}(x_{1})-m^{\mathfrak{b}}(x_{2}))^{2}}-\frac{1}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}\right).

We will consistently use the following conventions

\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})\equiv\alpha:=\widehat{\alpha}_{++}+\widehat{\alpha}_{--}-\widehat{\alpha}_{+-}-\widehat{\alpha}_{-+},\ \ \beta(x_{1},x_{2})\equiv\beta:=\widehat{\beta}_{++}+\widehat{\beta}_{--}-\widehat{\beta}_{+-}-\widehat{\beta}_{-+}.

(3.6)

The main results are the following two theorems. The first theorem establishes the CLT for the LSS on the global scale when
\eta_{0}\asymp 1.

Theorem 3.2

(Global CLT)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds. For
E\in\mathbf{R}
and
\eta_{0}\asymp 1,
the random vector
(Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}
in (
3.3
) follows that
(Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}\simeq\left(\mathscr{G}_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}},

where
\left(\mathscr{G}_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}
is a Gaussian random vector with mean and covariance functions defined as

\mathbb{E}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)b^{\mathfrak{+}}(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)b^{\mathfrak{-}}(x)\mathrm{d}x\right),

(3.7)

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{G}_{j}\right)=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f_{i}\left(x_{1}\right)f_{j}\left(x_{2}\right)\alpha\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{f_{i}\left(x_{1}\right)f_{j}\left(x_{2}\right)}\beta\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2},

(3.8)

where
b^{\pm}(x),\alpha(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\beta(x_{1},x_{2})
are defined between (
3.4
) and (
3.6
) and we assume their limits exist.

Remark 3.3
.

We point out that analogous results have been stated in the comparable regime (i.e.,
\alpha=1
in (
1.2
)) in
[
67
]
under stronger assumptions on the test functions. Even though the statements are different, we notice that the kernels of the covariance functions in (
3.8
) are essentially the same with the one in
[
67
]
using a contour integral representation which is an intermediate step in our calculation; see (
7.17
) below. Compared to the contour integral form, (
3.8
) eases the numerical approximations.

Remark 3.4
.

Several remarks are in order. First, we notice that the construction in (
3.2
) involves using a function with compact support so that the test functions needed to be well constructed. For example, we cannot directly use
g(x)=x.
However, this issue can be easily addressed using the following construction. For some
h(x)\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
we define

g(x)=h(x)\mathcal{K}(x),

(3.9)

where
\mathcal{K}(x)
is a mollifier that for some fixed small constant
a>0
and large constant
b

\mathcal{K}(x)\equiv\mathcal{K}_{a,b}(x):=\begin{cases}0&|x-b|\geq a\\
1&|x|\leq b\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{a^{2}}-\frac{1}{a^{2}-(x+b)^{2}}\right)&-(b+a)<x<-b\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{a^{2}}-\frac{1}{a^{2}-(x-b)^{2}}\right)&b<x<b+a\end{cases}.

(3.10)

It is then straightforward to check that the above construction will generate a
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}
function which preserves the properties of
h(x)
, e.g., when dealing with Theorem
3.2
, we can let
b
be large enough such that the effects of test functions
g(x)
on eigenvalues are identical with
h(x)
. Second, we believe that the condition
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{2}
can be weakened to the class of
\mathcal{C}^{1,a,b}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right):f^{\prime}\right.
is
a
Hölder continuous uniformly in
x
, and
|f(x)|+\left|f^{\prime}(x)\right|\leqslant C(1+|x|)^{-(1+b)}
for some constant
\left.C>0\right\}
following
[
30
,
61
]
. We will pursue this direction in the future works. Third, in the current paper, for simplicity, we assume that
\Sigma
is diagonal. However, such an assumption can be moved with additional technical efforts following
[
61
]
. More specifically, we can assume that
\Sigma^{1/2}
admits
\Sigma=O^{*}\Lambda O
where
O
is orthogonal and
\Lambda
is diagonal. Consequently, we will need to work with
\Lambda^{1/2}OXX^{*}O^{*}\Lambda^{1/2}.
Since this is out of the scope of the current paper, we will consider this generalization in the future works.

The above theorem establishes the joint distribution for LSS indexed by different test functions on the global scale. In general, the mean and covariance functions depend on the test functions,
\phi,

\Sigma,
the 4th cumulant,
E
and
\eta_{0}.
For some properly chosen test functions and
\Sigma,
the mean and covariances can be largely simplified and some explicit formulas could be obtained. In the following corollary, based on the above results, we can obtain the asymptotic normality for some individual specific test functions when
\Sigma=I
and
\eta_{0}=1.
These will be used in Section
4
regarding the statistical applications.

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds with
\Sigma=I.

(1).

For
E\in\mathbf{R}
and
\eta_{0}=1,
we have that

\frac{Z_{1,E}(f)-\mathsf{M}(f)}{\sqrt{\mathsf{V}(f)}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(3.11)

where
\mathsf{M}(f)
and
\mathsf{V}(f)
are defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}(f)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{r\downarrow 1}\frac{-1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|\xi|=1}f\left(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}+\xi+\frac{1}{\xi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\xi+\frac{1}{r}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\xi-\frac{1}{r}}\right)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle+\kappa_{4}\frac{-1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|\xi|=1}f(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}+\xi+\frac{1}{\xi})\left(-\frac{1}{\xi^{3}}\right)\mathrm{d}\xi,

(3.12)

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}(f)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{2}>r_{1}\\
r_{1},r_{2}\downarrow 1\end{subarray}}-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\oint_{|\xi_{1}|=1}\oint_{|\xi_{2}|=1}\frac{f(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{1}^{-1})f(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{2}^{-1})}{(\xi_{1}-r_{2}/r_{1}\xi_{2})^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa_{4}}{4\pi^{2}}\left(\oint_{|\xi|=1}f(\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}+\xi+\xi^{-1})\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{2}.

(3.13)

(2).

The formulas in (
(1).
) and (
(1).
) can be further simplified for some commonly used test functions and properly parametrized
E.
In particular, for
E=\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}-c
for some constant
c>0,
when
h_{1}(x)=x
in (
3.9
) and the associated
f_{1}(x)=(x-E)\mathcal{K}(x-E)
, we have that

\mathsf{M}(f_{1})=0,\ \mathsf{V}(f_{1})=2+\kappa_{4}.

Moreover, for
E=\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}-c
for some constant
c>0,
when
h_{2}(x)=x^{2}
in (
3.9
) and the associated
f_{2}(x)=(x-E)^{2}\mathcal{K}(x-E)
, we have that

\mathsf{M}(f_{2})=1+\kappa_{4},\ \mathsf{V}(f_{2})=4+4c^{2}(\kappa_{4}+2).

Finally, for
E=\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}-(t+t^{-1})
for some constant
t>1,
when
h_{3}(x)=\log x
in (
3.9
) and the associated
f_{3}(x)=\log(x-E)\mathcal{K}(x-E)
, we have that

\mathsf{M}(f_{3})=\frac{1}{2}\log(1-t^{-2})-\frac{\kappa_{4}}{2t^{2}},\ \mathsf{V}(f_{3})=2(\log t-\log(t-t^{-1}))+\frac{\kappa_{4}}{t^{2}}.

Remark 3.6
.

In addition to the mean and covariance functions, in general, the second term of the right-hand side of (
3.3
) needs to be computed numerically. However when
\Sigma=I,
since the density function of MP can be made explicit as in (
2.5
), this term can be further simplified for some test functions. Especially, for
f_{1}(x)
,
f_{2}(x)
and
E=\phi^{1/2}+\phi^{-1/2}-c
considered in (2) of Corollary
3.5
, we see from (
2.5
) that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\varrho(x)=c-\phi^{-1/2},\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{2}(x)\mathrm{d}\varrho(x)=1+c^{2}-2c\phi^{-1/2}+\phi^{-1}.

(3.14)

3.2
Local CLTs under (
1.2
)

The second theorem establishes the CLT for the LSS on the local scale when
\eta_{0}\ll 1.
Denote

\kappa\equiv\kappa(E):=\min\left\{|E-\gamma_{-}|,|E-\gamma_{+}|\right\}.

Theorem 3.7

(Local CLT)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds. Fix
E\in\mathbf{R}.
For some small constants
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}>0
,
\eta_{0}\leq n^{-\tau_{1}}
and
\eta_{0}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta_{0}}\geq n^{-1+\tau_{2}},
the random vector
(Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}
in (
3.3
) follows that
(Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}\simeq\left(\mathscr{G}_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}},

where
\left(\mathscr{G}_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\mathsf{K}}
is a Gaussian random vector with the mean and covariance functions defined as

\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)b_{1}^{\mathfrak{+}}(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)b_{1}^{\mathfrak{-}}(x)\mathrm{d}x\right),

(3.15)

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{G}_{j}\right)=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{f_{i}\left(x_{1}\right)f_{j}\left(x_{2}\right)}\beta\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2},

(3.16)

where
b_{1}^{\pm}(x)
and
\beta(x_{1},x_{2})
are defined in (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) and we assume their limits exist.

Remark 3.8
.

Two remarks are in order. First, in the comparable regime when
p\asymp n,
similar results have been obtained for a single test function in
[
43
]
with additional assumptions on
\Sigma
that
|1+\phi^{-1/2}m\sigma_{i}|
is bounded from below to ensure the square root behavior of the LSD near the edges; see Assumption 8.3 therein. However,
these conditions will be automatically satisfied under (
1.2
). Second, the proof
[
43
]
relies on a characteristic function approach. It is not clear how easily it can be generalized to study the multiple functions setting. We will utilize a different approach; see Section
3.3
for more details.

The above theorem shows that the LSS converges to some Gaussian processes who mean and covariance functions can be explicitly identified on the local scale. Compared to Theorem
3.2
, we find that when
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1),
the results do not reply on
\kappa_{4}
which makes it possible to create some moment free statistics to test (
1.1
). We illustrate this significant difference between the local and global CLTs in Figure
2
.

(a)

Global CLTs.

(b)

Local CLTs.

Figure 2:
Empirical Cumulative Distribution Functions (ECDFs) for global and local CLTs with different
\kappa_{4}
. We consider two different distributions for
x_{ij}:
the Gaussian distribution with
\kappa_{4}=0
and the two-point distribution that
\frac{1}{3}\delta_{\sqrt{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\delta_{-1/\sqrt{2}}
with
\kappa_{4}=-3/2.
We can conclude that for the CLTs with
\kappa_{4}=0
and
\kappa_{4}\neq 0
are very different on the scale that
\eta_{0}\asymp 1
but identical on the scale that
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1).
Here we choose a single test function that
h(x)=x
in (
3.9
),
n=400,\phi=100,

\eta_{0}=1
for global CLTs and
\eta_{0}=n^{-1/4}
for local CLTs, and the results are reported based on 1,000 repetitions.

Next, we show that when
E
is properly chosen, the formulas in Theorem
3.7
can be further simplified.

Corollary 3.9
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
3.7
hold. Recall the function
g_{i}(x)
in (
3.2
). Then the followings holds.

(1).

If
E
lies in the bulk of
\varrho
in the sense that
E\in(\gamma_{-}+\tau^{\prime},\gamma_{+}-\tau^{\prime})
for some fixed small constant
\tau^{\prime}>0,
then (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) can be further simplified as
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}_{i}=0
and

\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{G}_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(g_{i}\left(x_{1}\right)-g_{i}\left(x_{2}\right)\right)\left(g_{j}\left(x_{1}\right)-g_{j}\left(x_{2}\right)\right)}{\left(x_{1}-x_{2}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

(2).

If
E=\gamma_{+},
then (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) can be further simplified as
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}_{i}=g_{i}(0)/4
if
g_{i}(0)
exists and

\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{G}_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(g_{i}\left(-x_{1}^{2}\right)-g_{i}\left(-x_{2}^{2}\right)\right)\left(g_{j}\left(-x_{1}^{2}\right)-g_{j}\left(-x_{2}^{2}\right)\right)}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

Similarly, if
E=\gamma_{-},
we have that
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}_{i}=g_{i}(0)/4
and

\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{G}_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(g_{i}\left(x_{1}^{2}\right)-g_{i}\left(x_{2}^{2}\right)\right)\left(g_{j}\left(x_{1}^{2}\right)-g_{j}\left(x_{2}^{2}\right)\right)}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

3.3
Proof strategies and routine

In this section, we give a sketch of the proof strategies.
We first review the results and methods used in the most related works
[
43
,
61
,
66
,
67
]
, and then discuss how to generalize their methods and highlight our novelties.

We emphasize that all of the aforementioned works focus on the regime that
p
and
n
are comparably large, i.e.,
\alpha=1
in (
1.2
), where the support of
\varrho
is bounded. Under this setup,
[
66
,
67
]
established the global CLTs when
\eta_{0}=1.
On the technical level,
[
66
,
67
]
follow and generalize the strategies developed in
[
8
,
5
]
. However, to our best knowledge, their strategies require the testing functions to be sufficiently smooth and cannot be applied to study the local CLTs. When
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1),

[
43
]
studied the local CLTs by studying the characteristic functions of the LSS for a single test functions, i.e.,
\mathsf{K}=1
in (
3.2
). Even though it is possible to generalize
[
43
]
to general
\mathsf{K},
we believe it is a nontrivial task considering the complicated form of characteristic functions for random vectors. Finally, in
[
61
]
, the author studies both the global and local CLTs for LSS of the eigenvectors whose mean part is always zero. We point out that the detailed analysis of the LSS for the eigenvalues and eigenvectors is quite different in terms of their tracial representation. For concreteness, while both statistics can be written as
\operatorname{Tr}f(Q)A,
the eigenvalue LSS corresponds to a full rank matrix
A=I
and the eigenvector LSS corresponds to a rank-one matrix
A=\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^{*}
for some deterministic vector
\mathbf{v}.

To prove the main results, we need to generalize the methods used in these works, especially
[
43
,
61
]
. Our first step is to decompose the LSS in (
3.3
) into a deterministic mean bias part and a random mean zero part in the sense that

Z_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i})=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}f_{i}\left(Q\right)-n\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{i}(x)\mathrm{d}\varrho(x)+\mathcal{Z}_{{\eta_{0}},E}(f_{i}):=\mathcal{M}_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i})+\mathcal{Z}_{{\eta_{0}},E}(f_{i}).

(3.17)

For the random part
\mathcal{Z}_{{\eta_{0}},E}(f_{i}),
similar to
[
61
]
, with the help of Helffer-Sjöstrand formula (c.f. Lemma
5.8
), we find that it suffices to understand distributions of
\mathcal{Y}(z_{i}):=\operatorname{Tr}R_{1}(z_{i})-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}R_{1}(z_{i}),\ R_{1}(z_{i})=(Q-z_{i})^{-1}
at multiple points
z_{i}=E_{i}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}\in\mathbb{C},1\leq i\leq l.
We now focus our explanation on
l=2.
According to Wick’s theorem (c.f. Lemma
5.11
), it suffices to prove that for any
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{Y}^{l_{1}}\left(z_{1}\right)\mathcal{Y}^{l_{2}}\left(z_{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=l_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{Y}^{l_{1}-1}\left(z_{1}\right)\mathcal{Y}^{l_{2}-1}\left(z_{2}\right)\right]\omega(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle+(l_{2}-1)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{Y}^{l_{1}}\left(z_{1}\right)\mathcal{Y}^{l_{2}-2}\left(z_{2}\right)\right]\omega(z_{1},z_{2})+\mathrm{o}(1),

(3.18)

where
\omega(z_{1},z_{2})
encodes the variances and covariance (i.e.,
\omega(z_{1},z_{1}),\omega(z_{2},z_{2})
and
\omega(z_{1},z_{2})
are respectively the variances and covariance.) To prove (
3.3
), we utilize the cumulant expansions (c.f. Lemma
5.7
) . Roughly speaking, we need to estimate the terms of the following form

\sum_{i,j}\sum_{k=0}^{3}\frac{1}{k!}\kappa_{k+1}\mathbb{E}\frac{\partial^{k}(\mathcal{Y}(z_{1})^{l_{1}}\mathcal{Y}(z_{2})^{l_{2}-1}(R_{1}(z_{2})\Sigma^{1/2}X)_{ij})}{\partial x_{ij}^{k}},

(3.19)

plus a negligible error term. A key ingredient is the local laws for
Q
under (
1.2
) (c.f. Theorem
5.5
) which we will prove in Appendix
A
. With the local laws, the detailed discussion for estimating (
3.19
) will be provided in the proof of Lemma
6.2
. We point out that in the actual result Theorem
6.1
, in order to unify the regimes
\eta_{0}\asymp 1
and
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1),
we need to work with
\eta_{i}\mathcal{Y}(z_{i}).

Armed with the distributions of the resolvents, we can proceed to establish the distributions for
\mathcal{Z}_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i})
using the integral representation as in (
7.3
). The arguments are similar to those in
[
43
,
61
]
while we need address some challenges in order to get our final results. We now elaborate this as follows. In contrast to our statements in Theorems
3.2
and
3.7
, the results stated in
[
43
]
are in a regional integral form which may not be friendly for numerical calculations. In
[
61
]
, the author simplifies the contour integral by decomposing the extended functions into three parts and each part can be well controlled (see Section 6.2 therein). However, such a strategy fails in the eigenvalue LSS setting under the setup
p\gg n
as in (
1.2
). Instead, we first use the complex Green’s theorem (c.f. Lemma
5.9
) to translate the regional integral to a contour integral and then refine the formulas by some finer controls as in (
7.18
). For the mean part
\mathcal{M}_{\eta_{0},E}(f_{i}),
the arguments are similar. Again using the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula (c.f. (
7.22
) and (
7.23
)), it suffices to estimate
\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{Tr}(R_{1})-pm_{p})
which can be calculated in the same way as
\mathcal{Y}(z).
The analysis is provided in Section
7.2
and the result is provided in (
7.34
).

We point out that there are two advantages of the proof strategies. One is that it unifies the regimes
\eta_{0}\asymp 1
and
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1).
The other is that it demonstrates why the
\kappa_{4}
dependence vanishes for local CLTs. More specifically, by using a simple change of variable (
7.20
), the terms involving
\kappa_{4}
can be shown to be dependent on
\eta_{0}
explicitly (c.f. (
7.1
)) so that they will disappear when
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1).
Finally, to facilitate statistical applications, in Corollaries
3.5
and
3.9
, we provide more compact formulas for some important examples.
Technically, for Corollary
3.5
, we use the change of variables from
[
67
]
to further calculate (
(1).
) and (
3.8
), and we follow
[
43
]
to simplify (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) depending on whether
E
is in the bulk or at edges.

4
Statistical applications

In this section, we consider statistical applications of our results in Theorems
3.2
and
3.7
and Corollaries
3.5
and
3.9
to hypothesis testings on large covariance matrices. Especially, we want to test whether the population covariance matrix
\Sigma
in (
1.4
) is equal to a given deterministic positive definite matrix
\Sigma_{0}.
This is an important problem in multivariate data analysis
[
2
]
and high dimensional statistics
[
62
]
. Without loss of generality, we can set
\Sigma_{0}=I
so that the hypothesis testing problem can be formulated as

\mathbf{H}_{0}:\ \Sigma=I.

(4.1)

4.1
Testing statistics and their asymptotic normality

Inspired by the above discussions in Section
1
and motivated by our results in Section
3.1
, we consider two classes of statistics, the
global statistics
and the
local statistics
. For the global statistics, following the research line of
[
67
]
, for some test functions, we utilize (
3.3
) at the edge
\gamma_{+}
with
\eta_{0}=1.
As mentioned earlier, the successful application of the global statistics requires the prior knowledge of the fourth cumulant of
x_{ij}.
If such information is unavailable, we can use the local statistics with
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{o}(1).

Recall (
3.10
). For concreteness and computational simplicity, in view of Corollaries
3.5
and
3.9
, we propose the following groups of statistics to test
\mathbf{H}_{0}
in (
4.1
):

1.

We consider the statistics based on the test function
h_{1}(x)=x
in (
3.9
) that

\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{1}(\lambda_{j}-\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}+c),\ \ \mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right).

2.

We consider the statistics based on the test function
h_{2}(x)=x^{2}
in (
3.9
) that

\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{2}(\lambda_{j}-\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}+c),\ \mathcal{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right).

3.

We consider the modified log-likelihood ratio test based on test function
h_{3}(x)=(x+c)-\log(x+c)
,
c>0
is some fixed small constant, in (
3.9
) that

\mathcal{T}_{3}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{3}(\lambda_{j}-\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}),\ \mathcal{T}_{3}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{3}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right).

4.

We consider the modified John’s statistic that

\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{g}}:=n^{2}\frac{\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{g}}}{(\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{g}})^{2}},\ \mathcal{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{l}}:=n^{2}\frac{\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{l}}}{(\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}})^{2}}.

Remark 4.1
.

Two remarks are in order. First, in the four groups of test functions, except for the third group to make the logarithm function valid,
c
can be zero in all other statistics. In fact, our statistics are robust again the choices of
c.
Second, for the local statistics, we propose statistics based on LSS evaluated at the right-most edge, i.e.,
E=\gamma_{+}
. According to Corollary
3.9
, one can also construct statistics for
E
exactly in the bulk or at the left-most edge. They will have similar performance to the current ones.

Then we establish the asymptotics of the proposed statistics under
\mathbf{H}_{0}
in (
4.1
). For notional convenience, in this section, we let
\varrho_{0}
be the measure associated with the null hypothesis (
4.1
) as in Remark
2.4
. For
1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 3,

\mathsf{m}_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{l}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{k_{1}}\left(\frac{x-\gamma_{+}}{\eta_{0}}\right)\mathrm{d}\varrho_{0}(x),\ \ \mathsf{v}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\mathrm{l}}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left(h_{k_{1}}(-x_{1}^{2})\mathcal{K}(-x_{1}^{2})-h_{k_{2}}(-x_{2}^{2})\mathcal{K}(-x_{2}^{2})\right)^{2}}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

If
k_{1}=k_{2},
we simply write
\mathsf{v}_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{l}}\equiv\mathsf{v}_{k_{1},k_{1}}^{\mathrm{l}}.
Moreover, we define

\mathsf{v}_{4}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\left(\frac{-2(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{\mathrm{l}})}{(\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}})^{3}},\frac{1}{(\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}})^{2}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{v}_{1}^{l}&\mathsf{v}_{1,2}^{\mathrm{l}}\\
\mathsf{v}_{1,2}^{\mathrm{l}}&\mathsf{v}_{2}^{l}\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{-2(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{\mathrm{l}})}{(\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}})^{3}},\frac{1}{(\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}})^{2}}\right)^{*}.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorems
3.2
and
3.7
hold. Then when the null hypothesis
\mathbf{H}_{0}
in (
4.1
) holds, we have that:

1.

For the global statistics, we have that

\mathsf{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{g}}-n(c-\phi^{-1/2})}{\sqrt{2+\kappa_{4}}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1),\ \mathsf{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{g}}-n(1+c^{2}-2c\phi^{-1/2}+\phi^{-1})-1-\kappa_{4}}{\sqrt{4+4c^{2}(\kappa_{4}+2)}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(4.2)

\mathsf{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{g}}-n(1+(c-2\phi^{-1/2})^{-2})-(\kappa_{4}+1)(c-2\phi^{-1/2})^{2}}{\sqrt{c^{-4}\left(4+4(\kappa_{4}+2)/c^{2})^{2}\right)}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(4.3)

and for
c=t+t^{-1},t>1

\mathsf{T}_{3}^{\mathrm{g}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{3}^{\mathrm{g}}-n\left[(c-\phi^{-1/2})+\int\log(x-\phi^{1/2}-\phi^{-1/2}+c)\mathrm{d}\varrho_{0}(x)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-t^{-2})+\kappa_{4}/2t^{2}}{\sqrt{(2+\kappa_{4})\left(1-2/t\right)+2\left(\log t-\log(t-1/t)\right)+\kappa_{4}/t^{2}}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(4.4)

2.

For the local statistics, we have that for
1\leq\ell\leq 3,

\mathsf{T}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{l}}-n\mathsf{m}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{l}}-h_{\ell}(0)/4}{\sqrt{\mathsf{v}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{l}}}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(4.5)

and

\mathsf{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{4}^{\mathrm{l}}-n(\mathsf{m}_{2}^{\mathrm{l}}+h_{2}(0)/4n)/(\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}}+h_{1}(0)/4n)^{2}}{\sqrt{\mathsf{v}_{4}^{\mathrm{l}}}}\simeq\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(4.6)

Before concluding this section, we conduct a few numerical simulations to illustrate the accuracy of our proposed statistics in Figures
3
and
4
. We can conclude that all the proposed statistics are accurate and robust against various different choices of parameters.

’s. From left to right are:

and

with

,

and the results are reported based on 1,000 repetitions.

\eta_{0}
’s. From left to right are:

,

,

with

and

and the results are reported based on 1,000 repetitions.

4.2
Power analysis of the proposed statistics

In this section, we analyze the power of statistics proposed in Section
4.1
under the alternative that

\mathbf{H}_{a}:\Sigma\neq I.

(4.7)

For simplicity, we denote the ESD of
\Sigma
under (
4.7
) as
\pi_{a}
and for
s=1,2,

\mathfrak{m}_{s}(\pi_{a})=\int x^{s}\pi_{a}(\mathrm{d}x).

(4.8)

Let
z_{1-\alpha/2}
be the
1-\alpha/2
quantitle of the
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
random variable. The power of our proposed statistics is summarized in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.3
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorems
3.2
and
3.7
hold. Given some type I error rate
\alpha,
under (
4.7
), we have that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathsf{T}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{sy}}|>z_{1-\alpha/2}\right)=1,

where
1\leq\ell\leq 4
and
\mathsf{sy}=\mathrm{g},\mathrm{l}
are defined in Corollary
4.2
, if the following conditions are satisfied for some divergent sequence
C_{\alpha}\equiv C_{\alpha}(n)\rightarrow\infty
:

1.

For the global statistics
\mathsf{sy}=\mathrm{g},
when
\ell=1,3,
we require

|\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi_{a})-1|>C_{\alpha}n^{-1}\phi^{-1/2},

(4.9)

and when
\ell=2,4

\left((\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi_{a})-1)^{2}{\phi}+c|\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi_{a})-1|\phi^{1/2}+|\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\pi_{a})^{2}-1|\right)>C_{\alpha}n^{-1}.

(4.10)

2.

For the local statistics
\mathsf{sy}=\mathrm{l},
we require (
4.10
) for all
\ell=1,2,3,4.

We conclude that our proposed statistics are powerful even for weak local alternatives once (
4.9
) or (
4.10
) are satisfied. These conditions can be easily satisfied by some commonly used alternatives. For example, we can consider the spiked covariance models in the sense that

\mathbf{H}_{a}:\Sigma=\epsilon I_{r}+I,

where
I_{r}
is a
r\times r
identity matrix. For this case, to make (
4.9
) valid, we only require that
\epsilon r>C_{\alpha}p^{-1/2}n^{-1/2}.
For another instance, we can consider the covariance matrices with different clusters in the sense that

\mathbf{H}_{a}:\Sigma=a\delta_{1+\epsilon}+(1-a)\delta_{1}.

(4.11)

In this setting, to satisfy (
4.9
), we only require
a\epsilon>C_{\alpha}p^{-1/2}n^{-1/2}.
The requirements are weaker than those used in the most relevant work
[
53
]
which utilizes global statistics based on the normalized matrix (
1.3
). This partially explains why we prefer (
1.4
) to (
1.3
).

Before concluding this section, we conduct a few numerical simulations to illustrate the powerfulness of our proposed statistics in Figures
5
under the alternative in (
4.11
). We can see that our proposed statistics are very powerful even for small values of
\epsilon.

(a)

(b)

Local testing statistics.

Simulated power for proposed statistics. Here we use Gaussian distributions for
x_{ij},

a=0.5
in (
4.11
),
\phi=100,n=400
and the other parameters
c
and
\eta_{0}
are chosen in the same way as in the captions of Figures
3
and
4
. The simulations are reported based on 1,000 repetitions.

4.3
Comparison with existing methods

In this section, we compare our proposed methods with several existing ones in the literature. For concreteness, we focus on

[
51
,
37
,
57
,
22
,
58
,
28
,
3
,
1
,
53
]
.

To evaluate the performance of these tests, and to discern the trade-off between Type I and Type II error rates, we employ the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve and the Area Under Curve (AUC) score. The ROC curve is a plot where the
x
-axis represents the Type I error rate, or the probability of falsely rejecting a true null hypothesis (also known as a false positive rate), and the
y
-axis represents the power of the statistical test, which is the true positive rate (the ability to correctly accept an alternative hypothesis when it is true). The AUC score then quantifies the overall ability of a test to maximize the true positive rate while minimizing the Type I error rate. It does this by measuring the total area under the ROC curve - the larger the area, the better the test is at balancing these two types of errors.

We report our simulation results in Figures
6
and
7
below. In addition to the aforementioned nine methods in the literature, we also study our proposed statistics. For concreteness, we use
\mathsf{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{g}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{l}}
with
c=0
and
\eta_{0}=n^{-1/4},
denoted as PM1 and PM2 in the simulations. For the alternative, we consider (
4.11
) with
a=0.5.

As shown in Figure
6(a)
, when
p\gg n
and
\epsilon=0.03
is small, only three methods demonstrate significant power while maintaining appropriate size control. These methods are: our methods PM1, PM2 and the method suggested by
[
53
]
(Qiu21) which coincides with our PM1 in this particular scenario. The remaining tests perform at a level that is largely indistinguishable from a random guess, indicated by an AUC score around
0.5
. Among the tests compared with unsatisfactory performance,
[
51
]
(Nagao73) is derived in the low dimensional regime when
p
is fixed. Moreover,
[
37
,
3
,
28
,
57
]
denoted as Wolf02, CLRT09, Fisher12, Srivastava05, respectively in the figures, are derived in the comparable regime
p\asymp n
. Further,
[
22
,
58
,
1
]
denoted as Chen10, Srivastava11, Ahmad15 in the figures, respectively require stronger alternatives for powerfulness. When
\epsilon
increases to 0.3, as shown in Figure
6(b)
, more methodologies like
[
58
,
22
,
28
,
3
]
show significant improvements while
[
53
]
and our two proposed methods are still among the best.

We emphasize again, except for our PM2, all the other methods require prior knowledge of
\kappa_{4}.
In Figure
7
, we examine the situation when wrongly estimated
\kappa_{4}
is used to the conduct tests. It turns out our PM2 which is independent of
\kappa_{4}
has the best performance.

(a)

\epsilon=0.03

(b)

\epsilon=0.3

Figure 6:
ROC curves assuming
\kappa_{4}=0
is known. Here we use Gaussian distribution for
x_{ij},

\phi=100,n=400
and the results are reported based on 1,000 repetitions. For the legends, the values inside the parentheses are the AUC scores.

Figure 7:
ROC curves assuming
\kappa_{4}\neq 0
is misspecified. Here we use
\frac{\sqrt{2}+1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta_{\sqrt{2}-1}+\frac{\sqrt{2}-1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta_{-1-\sqrt{2}}
with true
\kappa_{4}=2.
In actual implementation, we used the misspecified estimate
\widehat{\kappa}_{4}=0.
Here
\epsilon=0.08,\phi=100,n=400
and the results are reported based on 1,000 repetitions.

5
Preliminaries, local laws and tools

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proof of the results in Sections
3.1
and
4.1
. In this section, we provide the key ingredients for our proofs. We first summarize the elementary properties of
m(z)
defined in Lemma
2.3
.
For
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}
, define

\kappa\equiv\kappa(z):=\min\left\{\left|\gamma_{+}-E\right|,\left|\gamma_{-}-E\right|\right\}.

Recall
\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi)=\int x\pi(\mathrm{d}x).
Moreover, for some fixed small constant
0<\tau<1,
denote the spectral parameter set as

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}\equiv\mathbf{S}(\tau):=\left\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}:|E-\mathfrak{m}_{1}(\pi)\phi^{1/2}|\leq\tau^{-1},n^{-1+\tau}\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau^{-1},|z|\geqslant\tau\right\}.

(5.1)

Lemma 5.1
.

Fix some small constant
0<\tau<1.
Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
2.3
and (
1.2
) hold. Then for all
z\in\mathbf{S}
in (
5.1
), we have that for some constant
c>0,

m(z)\asymp 1,\ \ \operatorname{Im}m(z)\geq c\eta,

(5.2)

and

\operatorname{Im}m(z)\asymp\begin{cases}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta},&E\in[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}]\\
\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}},&E\notin[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}].\end{cases}

(5.3)

Moreover, for its derivative
m^{\prime},
we have that

m^{\prime}\asymp\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}.

(5.4)

Proof.

See Appendices
B.1
and
B.2
.
∎

Let
m_{p}(z)
be the limit of Stieltjes transform of
\mathcal{Q}.
Since
Q
and
\mathcal{Q}
share the same non-zero eigenvalues, for
m(z)
defined in (
2.4
), we have that

m(z)=-\frac{1-{\phi}}{z}+{\phi}m_{p}(z).

(5.5)

Further, we use
\rho_{p}
to denote the measure induced by
m_{p}(z).
In the following lemma, we summarize some important identities.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
m(z)
defined in (
2.6
) and
m_{p}(z)
defined in (
5.5
), we have the following identities

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}=

\displaystyle-z+\frac{\phi}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}}{1+\phi^{-1/2}m\sigma_{i}},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z}=

\displaystyle-m+\frac{\phi}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}m}{z(1+\phi^{-1/2}m\sigma_{i})}=-m+\frac{\phi}{pz}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{1+\phi^{-1/2}m\sigma_{i}}\right),

\displaystyle m_{p}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi}\left(m+\frac{1-\phi}{z}\right)=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{z(1+\phi^{-1/2}m\sigma_{i})},

and

1-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\phi^{1/2}\sigma_{i}}{z\left(1+\phi^{-1/2}{m}(z)\sigma_{i}\right)^{2}}=-\frac{{m}(z)}{z{m}^{\prime}(z)}.

(5.7)

Proof.

(
5.6
) follows directly from (
2.6
) and (
5.5
). To see (
5.7
), we can rewrite (
2.6
) as

1+zm(z)=\phi-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\phi}{1+\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}m(z)}.

(5.8)

Then taking derivative on both sides of the above equation, we obtain that

m(z)+zm^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\phi^{1/2}\sigma_{i}m^{\prime}(z)}{(1+\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}m(z))^{2}}.

(5.9)

This proves (
5.7
).
∎

Next, as in
[
25
,
35
,
38
,
61
]
, we see that it is more convenient to work with some linearization of the matrix
X.
For notational convenience, we introduce the following conventions.

Definition 5.3
.

We introduce the index sets

\mathcal{I}_{1}:=\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket,\quad\mathcal{I}_{2}:=\llbracket p+1,p+n\rrbracket,\quad\mathcal{I}:=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}=\llbracket 1,p+n\rrbracket.

We consistently use the letters
i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{1},\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
, and
s,t\in\mathcal{I}
. With the above notations, we can label the indices of the matrices according to

X=\left(X_{i\mu}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{1},\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}\right),\quad\Sigma=\left(\Sigma_{ij}:i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\right).

The following linearizing block matrices will play important roles in our calculations. For
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},
define

G(z)\equiv G(z,\Sigma,X):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\Sigma^{-1}&X\\
X^{*}&-zI_{n}\end{array}\right)^{-1}.

(5.10)

Moreover, for
m(z)
defined in (
2.4
), we denote

\Pi(z)\equiv\Pi(z,\Sigma):=-z^{-1}\left(I_{p}+\phi^{-1/2}m(z)\Sigma\right)^{-1}.

(5.11)

For
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},
we define the resolvents of
Q
and
\mathcal{Q}
respectively as

R_{1}(z):=\left(\Sigma^{1/2}XX^{*}\Sigma^{1/2}-z\right)^{-1},\ R_{2}(z):=\left(X^{*}\Sigma X-z\right)^{-1}.

(5.12)

We will see later from Theorem
5.5
that
\Pi(z)
is the deterministic counterpart of
R_{1}(z).
Denote the control parameter
\Psi(z)
as

\Psi(z):=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Im}m(z)}{n\eta}}+\frac{1}{n\eta}.

(5.13)

We now state the main results on the local laws which provide precise controls on
G,R_{1}
and
R_{2}.
We will need the following notion of stochastic domination which is commonly used in modern random matrix theory
[
27
]
.

Definition 5.4

(Stochastic domination)
.

(i) Let

A=\left(A^{(n)}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),\quad B=\left(B^{(n)}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),

be two families of nonnegative random variables, where
U^{(n)}
is a possibly
n
-dependent parameter set. We say
A
is stochastically dominated by
B
, uniformly in
u
, if for any fixed (small)
\epsilon>0
and (large)
D>0
,

\sup_{u\in U^{(n)}}\mathbb{P}\left(A^{(n)}(u)>n^{\epsilon}B^{(n)}(u)\right)\leq n^{-D},

for large enough
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon,D)
, and we shall use the notation
A\prec B
. Throughout this paper, the stochastic domination will always be uniform in all parameters that are not explicitly fixed, such as the matrix indices and the spectral parameter
z
. Throughout the paper, even for negative or complex random variables, we also write
A\prec B
or
A=\mathrm{O}_{\prec}(B)
for
|A|\prec B
.

(ii) We say an event
\Xi
holds with high probability if for any constant
D>0
,
\mathbb{P}(\Xi)\geq 1-n^{-D}
for large enough
n
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
hold. For all
z\in\mathbf{S}
defined in (
5.1
) uniformly and any deterministic vectors
\mathbf{u}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\mathbf{v}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},k=1,2,
we have that:

(1).

For the resolvents of
Q
and
\mathcal{Q}
defined in (
5.12
), we have that

\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}R_{1}(z)\mathbf{u}_{2}=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}\Pi(z)\mathbf{u}_{2}+\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\phi^{-1}\Psi(z)\right),

(5.14)

where
\Pi(z)
is defined in (
5.11
) and
\Psi(z)
is defined in (
5.13
). Moreover, we have that

\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}R_{2}(z)\mathbf{v}_{2}=m(z)\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\Psi(z)\right).

(5.15)

(2).

Denote the normalized resolvents as

m_{1n}(z)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(R_{1}(z))_{ii},\ m_{2n}(z)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(R_{2}(z))_{ii}.

We have that

m_{1n}(z)=-\frac{1}{zp}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{1+\sigma_{i}\phi^{-1/2}m(z)}+\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left((p\eta)^{-1}\right),

(5.16)

and

m_{2n}(z)=m(z)+\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left((n\eta)^{-1}\right).

(5.17)

(3).

Recall the conventions for the indices in Definition
5.3
. For
G(z)
defined in (
5.10
), we have that uniformly in
\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
and
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}

G_{i\mu}(z)=\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\phi^{-1/4}\Psi(z)\right).

(5.18)

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Several remarks are in order. First, the counterparts of the results for
\alpha=1
have been proved in
[
35
]
. In their setting, since
p
and
n
are comparably large, the results are essentially stated for the linearization matrix
G(z)
. However, as can be seen from our results in Theorem
5.5
, the convergence rates of different blocks of
G(z)
vary according to their sizes so that we need to state the results separately by carefully keeping tracking of
p,n
and
\phi
. Second, for the results in
[
35
]
, as mentioned in Remark
2.6
, since there may exist several bulk components so that we need more regularity conditions as discussed in Remark
3.4
. In our setting (
1.2
), most of these conditions are satisfied automatically. In fact, as can be concluded from the proofs of Theorem
5.5
, the arguments applies to all
0<\alpha<\infty
so that the results of
[
35
]
can be actually recovered by our results. Third, in the current paper, we only need the entry-wise control (
5.18
) for the off-diagonal block terms of the matrix
G(z)
in (
5.10
). In fact, with additional technical efforts, we can show that for all
\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
and
\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n},
where
\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{1}
is the natural embedding of
\mathbf{u}_{1}
and
\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
is that of
\mathbf{v}_{1},
we have that

\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{1}^{*}G(z)\bar{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\phi^{-1/4}\Psi(z)\right).

Before concluding this section, we collect some useful formulas which will be used in our proof. The following cumulant expansion formula plays a crucial role in our calculations which have been used in calculating the CLTs for various random matrix models, to name but a few,
[
12
,
11
,
43
,
61
]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and let
h
be a real-valued random variable with finite moments up to order
2\ell+2.
Moreover, let
f
be a complex-valued smooth function that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}(\mathbb{R}).
Let
\kappa_{k}
be the
k
-th cumulant of
h,
given by
\kappa_{k}:=(-\mathrm{i})^{k}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log\mathbb{E}e^{\mathrm{i}th}|_{t=0}.
Then we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[hf(h)\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\frac{1}{k!}\kappa_{k+1}\mathbb{E}f^{(k)}(h)+R_{\ell+1},

where the error term satisfies

|R_{\ell+1}|\leq C_{\ell}\mathbb{E}|h|^{\ell+2}\sup_{|x|\leq M}|f^{(\ell+1)}(x)|+C_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[|h|^{\ell+2}\mathbf{1}_{|h|>M}\right]\|f^{(\ell+1)}(x)\|_{\infty},

for some constant
C_{\ell}>0
and
M>0
is an arbitrary fixed cutoff.

Proof.

See Proposition 3.1 of
[
47
]
.
∎

Then we introduce the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula which will connect the LSS with the resolvents.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r+1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\chi(y)
be a smooth cutoff function with bounded support. Define its almost-analytic extension of degree
r
as

\widetilde{f}_{r}(x+\mathrm{i}y):=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\frac{1}{k!}(\mathrm{i}y)^{k}f^{(k)}(x).

Let
\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C};[0,1])
be a smooth cutoff function. Then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\chi(\lambda)=1
we have

f(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\bar{\partial}\left(\tilde{f}_{r}(z)\chi(z)\right)}{\lambda-z}\mathrm{~d}^{2}z,\ z=x+\mathrm{i}y,

where
\bar{\partial}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)
is the antiholomorphic derivative and
\mathrm{d}^{2}z
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}.

Proof.

See
[
23
]
or
[
13
, Section 1.13.3]
.
∎

Next, we record the Green’s theorem in the complex form which can be used to transfer the region integral to contour integral.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
B(z,\bar{z})
is continuous and has continuous partial derivatives in a region
\mathcal{R}
and on its boundary. We have the following complex form of Green’s theorem

\oint_{\partial\mathcal{R}}B(z,\bar{z})\mathrm{d}z=2\mathrm{i}\iint_{\mathcal{R}}\frac{\partial B}{\partial\bar{z}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

More specifically, when
B(z,\bar{z})=F(z,\bar{z})h(z)
with
h(z)
being holomorphic in
\mathcal{R}
and on
\partial\mathcal{R}
, we have

\oint_{\partial\mathcal{R}}B(z,\bar{z})\mathrm{d}z=\oint_{\partial\mathcal{R}}F(z,\bar{z})h(z)\mathrm{d}z=2\mathrm{i}\iint_{\mathcal{R}}\frac{\partial F(z,\bar{z})}{\partial\bar{z}}h(z)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Proof.

See Theorem 4.10 of
[
56
]
.
∎

Then we introduce the Sokhotski-Plemelj lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
f
be a complex-valued function which can be continuously extended to the real line, and let

a<0<b
be some real numbers. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{a}^{b}{\frac{f(x)}{x\pm\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}x=\mp\mathrm{i}\pi f(0)+{\mathcal{P}}\int_{a}^{b}{\frac{f(x)}{x}}\mathrm{d}x,

where
\mathcal{P}
denotes the Cauchy principal value.

Proof.

See Chapter 12 of
[
50
]
.
∎

Finally, we provide the Wick’s probability theorem which can be used to characterize the multivariate Gaussian distribution.

Lemma 5.11
.

Suppose
(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k})^{*}
follows mean zero multivariate Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\Lambda),
and let
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\in\{y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k}\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},
then

\mathbb{E}(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n})=\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in\mathrm{P}_{n}^{2}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in p}\mathbb{E}x_{i}x_{j}=\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in\mathrm{P}_{n}^{2}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathsf{p}}\operatorname{Cov}(x_{i},x_{j}),

where
\mathrm{P}_{n}^{2}
constains all distinct ways of partitioning
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
into pairs
\{i,j\}.
Equivalently, by combining the terms that are the same, we have that for
r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{k}\in\mathbb{N},

\mathbb{E}(y_{1}^{r_{1}}y_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots y_{k}^{r_{k}})=(r_{1}-1)\Lambda_{11}\mathbb{E}(y_{1}^{r_{1}-2}y_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots y_{k}^{r_{k}})+\sum_{i=2}^{k}r_{i}\Lambda_{1i}\mathbb{E}(y_{1}^{r_{1}}y_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots y_{k}^{r_{k}}/(y_{1}y_{i})).

Especially, when
k=2,
we have that

\mathbb{E}y_{i}^{s}y_{j}^{t}=(s-1)\Lambda_{ii}\mathbb{E}y_{i}^{s-2}y_{j}^{t}+t\Lambda_{ij}\mathbb{E}y_{i}^{s-1}y_{j}^{t-1}.

Proof.

See Section 1.2 of
[
70
]
.
∎

6
CLTs for the resolvents

We first prepare some notations. Denote

\mathcal{Y}(z_{s})=(1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{{R_{1}}}(z_{s}),s=1,2,\ldots,l.

(6.1)

Denote

\widehat{\alpha}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right):=\kappa_{4}\phi\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{1}\partial z_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{\left(1+\phi^{-1/2}{m}^{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{1})\sigma_{i}\right)\left(1+\phi^{-1/2}{m}^{\mathfrak{b}}(z_{2})\sigma_{i}\right)}\right),

\widehat{\beta}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right):=2\left(\frac{(m)^{\prime}(z_{1})(m)^{\prime}(z_{2})}{(m(z_{1})-m(z_{2}))^{2}}-\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\right).

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose
X
and
\Sigma
satisfy Assumptions
2.1
. Fix any
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in\mathbf{S}
, we have that
\left(\eta_{1}\mathcal{Y}(z_{1}),\ldots,\eta_{l}\mathcal{Y}(z_{l})\right)\simeq\left(\mathscr{G}(z_{1}),\ldots,\mathscr{G}(z_{l})\right),
where
\left(\mathscr{G}(z_{1}),\ldots,\mathscr{G}(z_{l})\right)
is a complex Gaussian vector with mean
\mathbf{0}
and covariances

\operatorname{Cov}\left(\mathscr{G}(z_{i}),\mathscr{G}(z_{j})\right)=\eta_{i}\eta_{j}\left[\hat{\alpha}(z_{i},z_{j})+\hat{\beta}(z_{i},z_{j})\right],\quad 1\leq i,j\leq l.

The proof of Theorem
6.1
replies on the following lemma. Denote

\mathsf{Re}:=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{l}|\eta_{i}|}\left(\frac{1}{n\min_{i=1}^{l}\{|\eta_{i}|\sqrt{\kappa_{i}+|\eta_{i}|}\}}+\frac{1}{n^{3/4}p^{1/4}\min_{i=1}^{l}\{\sqrt{|\eta_{i}|(\kappa_{i}+|\eta_{i}|)}\}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Lemma 6.2
.

Suppose
X
and
\Sigma
satisfy Assumptions
2.1
. Fix any
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in\mathbf{S},\ i=1,\ldots,l
with imaginary part
\geq n^{-1+\varepsilon}
for some positive
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{Y}\left(z_{i}\right)\right]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum\prod\omega\left(z_{s},z_{t}\right)+\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\mathsf{Re}\right),&\text{ if }l\in 2\mathbb{N}\\
\mathrm{O}_{\prec}\left(\mathsf{Re}\right),&\text{ otherwise }\end{array}.\right.

(6.2)

Here we denoted
\omega\left(z_{s},z_{t}\right):=\widehat{\alpha}\left(z_{s},z_{t}\right)+\widehat{\beta}\left(z_{s},z_{t}\right)
, and
\sum\prod
means summing over all distinct ways of partitioning of indices into pairs.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

The proof follows directly from Lemmas
6.2
,
5.11
and the Portmanteau theorem.
∎

6.1
Proof of Lemma
6.2

For simplicity, we focus on
l=2
and then discuss how to handle general
l
in the end of the proof. In this setting, according to Lemma
5.11
, it suffices to prove (
3.3
). Furthermore, using (
6.1
) and the identity that for any two random variables
a
and
b,

\mathbb{E}[a(1-\mathbb{E})b]=\mathbb{E}[(1-\mathbb{E})a(1-\mathbb{E})b]=\mathbb{E}[b(1-\mathbb{E})a],
we see it suffices to control

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}\right\}.

Using the identity
zR_{1}(z)=R_{1}(z)Q-I
, Lemma
5.7
and the convention
Y=\Sigma^{1/2}X
, we have that

\displaystyle z_{2}\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}\right\}

(6.3)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({{R_{1}}(z_{2})Q})_{ii}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({{R_{1}}(z_{2})\Sigma^{1/2}XX^{*}\Sigma^{1/2}})_{ii}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}x_{ij}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}}{\sqrt{pn}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\partial(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}}{\partial x_{ij}}\right\}+\mathsf{L}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}}{\sqrt{pn}}\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]\frac{\partial({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}}{\partial x_{ij}}\right\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}}{\sqrt{pn}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\partial((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}{\partial x_{ij}}({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}\right\}+\mathsf{L}_{i}

\displaystyle=:\mathsf{A}_{i}+\mathsf{B}_{i}+\mathsf{L}_{i},

where

\mathsf{L}_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\left[\sum_{k=2}^{l_{0}}\frac{1}{k!}\kappa_{k+1}\mathbb{E}\frac{\partial^{k}\left([{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}\right)}{\partial x_{ij}^{k}}+\varepsilon_{l_{0}+1}^{(ij)}\right].

with
\varepsilon
’s being the remainder term. By the Lemma
5.7
and the fact that
\kappa_{k+1}(x_{ij})\sim(np)^{-\frac{k+1}{4}}
, we choose
l_{0}=4
so that
\sum_{j}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\varepsilon_{l_{0}+1}^{(ij)}=\mathrm{o}(p^{-1})
.

Before proceeding to the control of
\mathsf{A}_{i},\mathsf{B}_{i}
and
\mathsf{L}_{i},
we first prepare some useful identities.

\frac{\partial\left({R_{1}}(z)\right)_{ab}}{\partial y_{ij}}=-\left({R_{1}}(z)\right)_{ai}\left(Y^{*}{R_{1}}(z)\right)_{jb}-\left({R_{1}}(z)Y\right)_{aj}({R_{1}}(z))_{ib},\quad\frac{\partial\left({R_{1}}(z)\right)_{ab}}{\partial x_{ij}}=\frac{\partial\left({R_{1}}(z)\right)_{ab}}{\partial y_{ij}}\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}.

(6.4)

\sum_{l=1}^{p}\frac{\partial({R_{1}}(z))_{ll}}{\partial x_{ij}}=-2\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}({R_{1}}(z)Y)_{ij}.

(6.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial({R_{1}}Y)_{ij}}{\partial x_{ij}}=

\displaystyle(R_{1}\Sigma^{1/2}E_{ij})_{ij}-[R_{1}(\Sigma^{1/2}E_{ij}X^{*}\Sigma^{1/2}+\Sigma^{1/2}XE_{ji}\Sigma^{1/2})R_{1}\Sigma^{1/2}X]_{ij}

(6.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}[({R_{1}})_{ii}-({R_{1}})_{ii}(Y^{*}R_{1}Y)_{jj}-(R_{1}Y)_{ij}(Y^{*}R_{1})_{ij}],

where
E_{ij}
is a
p\times n
matrix whose only nonzero entry is one and in the (
i,j
) position.

For
\mathsf{A}_{i},
using (
6.4
)–
6.6
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{i}=

\displaystyle z_{2}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E}){\left[({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{i}}}{\sqrt{pn}}\mathbb{E}\left\{(1-\mathbb{E})[{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}}]\frac{\partial({R_{1}}(z_{2})Y)_{ij}}{\partial x_{ij}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})[({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left(Y^{*}{{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y\right)_{jj}({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}-({{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y)_{ij}({{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y)_{ij}]\right\}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{1}+(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{2}+(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{3}

where
(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{k},1\leq k\leq 3,
are defined as

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E}){\left[({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{2}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})[\left(Y^{*}{{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y\right)_{jj}({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}]\right\}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{3}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})[({{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y)_{ij}({{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y)_{ij}]\right\}

Note for
(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{1}=\phi^{-1/2}\sigma_{i}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E}){\left[({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}\right]}\right\}
, since it has the same structure in definition of
\mathsf{A}_{i}
, we will merge it in the end. For
(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{2}
, using Theorem
5.5
, we have that

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{i})_{2}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})[\left(Y^{*}{{R_{1}}(z_{2})}Y\right)_{jj}({{R_{1}}(z_{2})})_{ii}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{{\sqrt{pn}}}\mathbb{E}\left\{((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})[\operatorname{Tr}({R_{1}}(z_{2})YY^{*})({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sigma_{i}\phi^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})\left(z_{2}p^{-1}({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}\right)\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2})\right]

\displaystyle-\sigma_{i}\phi^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})\left[({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\phi^{1/2}\left(-z_{2}m_{p}(z_{2})-1\right)\mathbb{E}\left[((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})\left(({R_{1}}(z_{2}))_{ii}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sigma_{i}\phi^{1/2}/(1+\phi^{-1/2}m(z_{2})\sigma_{i})\mathbb{E}\left[((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{1}))^{l_{1}}((1-\mathbb{E})\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2}))^{l_{2}-1}(1-\mathbb{E})\left(p^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}{R_{1}}(z_{2})\right)\right]
